      1:{label:'Needs development',title:'The pressure of a weekend call shaped too many of your decisions.',text:'The awareness clock, the expectedness determination, and the documentation integrity are three distinct obligations — review the feedback on each one.'},
      0:{label:'Critical concern',title:'An incorrectly timed report, a questionable expectedness classification, and a falsified awareness date.',text:'Each of these would be a separate finding in an inspection. Weekend pressure explains why these errors happen; it does not make them defensible.'}
    }
  },

  /* ── SCENARIO 4: RA — The Previous CRC’s Files ── */
  {
    id:'predecessor-files', role:'ra',
    topic:'Data Integrity · Delegation · Research Integrity',
    title:'The Previous CRC’s Files',
    desc:'You’re three months in. While filing documents, you discover your predecessor signed checklists for visits she didn’t attend.',
    scenes:3, ref:'[SOP-CR-002](/sops/cr-002), 014, 015', live:true,
    intro:'You replaced Émilie, a well-liked CRC who left for another institution. Your PI trained you personally and frequently mentions how smoothly she ran the study. A monitoring visit is next week.',
    learner:{name:'Karim',role:'Research Assistant, 3 months on the job. Preparing the ISF for a monitoring visit next week.'},
    npc:{name:'Dr. Bhatt',role:'PI. Supportive, busy, thinks highly of Émilie. Not trying to cover anything up — genuinely believes there’s a simple explanation.'},
    pressurePoints:[
      {label:'Pressure point 1 — The discovery',
       narration:'While organizing the ISF, you notice three consent checklists signed by Émilie during a two-week period when she was on vacation and Dr. Bhatt was the only person on-site. You mention it to Dr. Bhatt.',
       speaker:'Dr. Bhatt',
       dialogue:'<p>Émilie was incredibly organized — she probably completed the checklists before she left and just dated them for the visit days. She always prepared everything in advance. I wouldn’t worry about it.</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'I hear you, but the dates don’t line up with the delegation log — she was listed as on leave. I think we need to look into who actually conducted those consents before the monitor sees this next week.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’re not accusing anyone — you’re identifying a documentation discrepancy that needs resolution before the monitoring visit. The investigation is straightforward: check who was on-site, whether consents were actually obtained, and whether the documentation can be corrected with a note-to-file. Identifying this proactively is the stronger position.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ALCOA+ · ICH E6(R3) §2.8.6 · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014) · [SOP-CR-015](/sops/cr-015)</span>'},
         {text:'That makes sense — she was very thorough. I’ll file them as they are.',quality:'fail',label:'Pre-dating consent documentation is falsification',
          feedback:'<p>A consent checklist documents that a specific person conducted a specific consent discussion on a specific date. If Émilie was on vacation, she did not conduct those discussions. Pre-completing and pre-dating consent documentation is falsification, even if the consents themselves were obtained properly by someone else. The monitoring visit next week will surface the same discrepancy.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ALCOA+ · ICH E6(R3) §2.8.6 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008) · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014)</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll make a note in my own files but I don’t want to create a problem right before the monitoring visit.',quality:'partial',label:'Self-protection without addressing the problem',
          feedback:'<p>Making a personal note protects you but doesn’t address the problem. The monitor will cross-reference the signatures against the delegation log and study calendar next week — exactly as you just did. If the discrepancy is discovered without any prior site investigation, it looks worse than if the site identified and addressed it proactively.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-015 · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014)</span>'}
       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 2 — The pattern',
       narration:'You look more carefully and find additional issues: one consent form where the participant signed three days before Émilie documented the discussion, and an eligibility checklist confirming a lab value that wasn’t available until two days after the confirmation date. You bring this to Dr. Bhatt.',
       speaker:'Dr. Bhatt',
       dialogue:'<p>Look, Émilie was with us for four years and she never had a single finding. These are probably just sloppy dating — she was juggling a lot at the end before she left. I think we document what we found and move on. No need to involve QA or anyone else.</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'You’re right — she was probably just rushing. I’ll write a note-to-file for each one and we’ll move on.',quality:'fail',label:'A pattern requires more than individual notes-to-file',
          feedback:'<p>What you’ve found is a pattern: consent checklists signed during vacation, mismatched dates, and a lab confirmation predating the result. This is no longer sloppy dating — it’s a pattern across multiple documents. A note-to-file for each individual finding without escalation treats a systemic issue as isolated mistakes.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · ICH E6(R3) §4 · MUHC Research Integrity Policy</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll document everything I found and present it to the monitor proactively during the visit.',quality:'partial',label:'Proactive, but without institutional support',
          feedback:'<p>Proactive disclosure is better than waiting for the monitor to find it. But presenting findings without first involving QA or the compliance office puts you in the position of reporting on your predecessor without institutional support. The monitor will ask what steps the site is taking — and “the RA found it and brought it to the visit” is not a CAPA.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · [SOP-CR-015](/sops/cr-015)</span>'},
         {text:'I think this goes beyond dating errors — there’s a pattern. I’d like to loop in QA or the research compliance office before the monitoring visit so the site has a documented response ready.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>What you’ve found may have an innocent explanation — but the pattern warrants formal review. Involving QA before the monitoring visit means the site can present the findings as self-identified with an investigation in progress. That is fundamentally different from having the monitor discover unaddressed discrepancies.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · [SOP-CR-002](/sops/cr-002) · MUHC Research Integrity Policy</span>'}
       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 3 — The personal cost',
       narration:'Dr. Bhatt pulls you aside.',
       speaker:'Dr. Bhatt',
       dialogue:'<p>Karim, I appreciate your diligence, but I want to be honest with you. If we escalate this formally, it could reflect poorly on my oversight of the study — these happened on my watch. And Émilie is a colleague. I’m not asking you to hide anything — I’m asking you to let me handle it quietly. I’ll review the files myself and make sure everything is in order for the monitor.</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'I understand — you know the study better than I do. I’ll leave it with you.',quality:'fail',label:'The person whose oversight is in question cannot be the sole reviewer',
          feedback:'<p>Dr. Bhatt is asking you to let the person whose oversight is in question investigate and resolve the findings alone. His concern about how it reflects on him is understandable — but is exactly why he should not be the sole person managing this. If the monitor later finds issues that were identified internally but not properly escalated, both his oversight and your awareness become part of the finding.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.3.1 · MUHC Research Integrity Policy · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014)</span>'},
         {text:'I understand this is uncomfortable, and I’m not trying to create a problem for you or Émilie. But I think the safest path for everyone — including you — is to have QA review this independently. That way the monitor sees that the site self-identified the issue and followed its own quality process.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’ve acknowledged Dr. Bhatt’s concern without conceding the point. Framing the escalation as protection for the PI — not as an accusation — is both accurate and effective. An independent QA review that finds documentation errors and a CAPA is a routine quality event. A monitor discovering the same pattern with no site response is a finding that questions PI oversight.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.3.1 · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014) · [SOP-CR-002](/sops/cr-002)</span>'},
         {text:'I won’t escalate it formally, but I’m going to document exactly what I found, when I found it, and that I raised it with you.',quality:'partial',label:'Self-protection without resolution',
          feedback:'<p>You’re protecting yourself, which is understandable. But a documented record of raising concerns that were then not acted on is not a good outcome for anyone. If the monitor finds the issues and sees that you identified them weeks earlier but the site took no action, the finding is worse.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · MUHC Research Integrity Policy</span>'}
       ]}
    ],
    endings:{
      3:{label:'Strong',title:'You identified a pattern, escalated properly, and navigated the personal dynamics professionally.',text:'Raising concerns about a colleague’s work — especially one your PI respects — is one of the hardest things in clinical research. You handled it without either backing down or making it adversarial.'},
      2:{label:'Competent with gaps',title:'You caught the problem and made some of the right moves.',text:'Review the feedback — the gap is usually between recognizing the issue and following through on the escalation.'},
      1:{label:'Needs development',title:'The personal dynamics shaped your decisions more than the evidence did.',text:'A respected predecessor, a supportive PI, a monitoring visit next week — these are the situations where documentation errors become institutional problems.'},
      0:{label:'Critical concern',title:'You found a pattern of discrepancies and chose not to pursue any of them.',text:'If the monitor identifies what you already identified, the site’s position is significantly worse than if it had self-reported. The personal discomfort of escalation is real, but it’s temporary.'}
    }
  },

  /* ── SCENARIO 5: PM — The Close-Out ── */
  {
    id:'close-out', role:'pm',
    topic:'Study Close-Out · Protocol Deviations · Consent',
    title:'The Close-Out',
    desc:'The sponsor needs the site file closed by end of June. Three open items that can’t be rushed.',
    scenes:3, ref:'[SOP-CR-016](/sops/cr-016), 026, 008', live:true,
    intro:'The last participant completed their final visit six weeks ago. The sponsor’s regulatory submission is in August and they need the site file closed by end of June.',
    learner:{name:'Nathalie',role:'Project Manager for a completed Phase III cardiovascular trial.'},
    npc:{name:'James Chen',role:'Sponsor Clinical Operations Lead. Managing close-out across 40 sites globally. Yours is one of the last.'},
    pressurePoints:[
      {label:'Pressure point 1 — The open deviations',
       narration:'James opens the call by reviewing the close-out checklist.',
       speaker:'James',
       dialogue:'<p>Your file is in good shape overall. I see six protocol deviations still open — five minor visit-window issues and one major eligibility deviation that’s been open for four months. For the five minor ones, can your PI sign the CAPA forms I sent? And for the major one — we’ve assessed it as having no impact on data integrity. Can we close it with that assessment?</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'If you’ve assessed the eligibility deviation as no impact, I’m comfortable closing it with your assessment on file.',quality:'fail',label:'The sponsor’s assessment doesn’t substitute for the PI’s',
          feedback:'<p>The sponsor’s assessment of impact does not substitute for the PI’s independent assessment, and it does not substitute for REB notification. A major eligibility deviation open for four months without a PI assessment or REB report is a compliance failure — closing it on the sponsor’s assessment alone adds another one.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-026 §5.3.3 · ICH E6(R3) §2.5.2</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll get the PI to sign everything and close them all out this week.',quality:'partial',label:'The minor ones are straightforward; the major one isn’t',
          feedback:'<p>Getting the PI to sign the minor CAPA forms quickly is fine. But treating the major eligibility deviation the same way — as a signature exercise — misses the point. The PI must review the case, document an independent impact assessment, and the REB must be notified. Rushing a major deviation through close-out is how incomplete CAPAs end up in the regulatory file.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-026 §5.3.3 · [SOP-CR-016](/sops/cr-016)</span>'},
         {text:'The five minor ones — I’ll get those signed this week. But the major eligibility deviation needs the PI’s own impact assessment and needs to go to the REB if it hasn’t already. I can’t close it on the sponsor’s assessment alone.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’ve separated the straightforward items from the one that requires more work. The major deviation has specific obligations: independent PI assessment, REB notification, and a documented CAPA — not just a signature. You can close the minor ones quickly and give James a realistic timeline for the major one.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-026 §5.3.3 · ICH E6(R3) §2.5.2 · [SOP-CR-016](/sops/cr-016)</span>'}
       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 2 — The unsigned report',
       narration:'James moves on to the monitoring visit reports.',
       speaker:'James',
       dialogue:'<p>The final monitoring visit report from February still doesn’t have the PI’s co-signature. Our CRA sent it three times. Can you get that signed by Friday?</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'Dr. Martin has told me he hasn’t reviewed it yet. I’ll schedule a 30-minute block with him this week specifically for this review, and I’ll let you know once he’s signed after reading it. If that pushes past Friday, I’ll give you a firm date.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’re being transparent about the reason for the delay, proposing a concrete solution — dedicated time for actual review — and offering a firm date rather than a vague promise. James will appreciate a realistic timeline more than another commitment you can’t keep.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.3.1 · [SOP-CR-013](/sops/cr-013) · [SOP-CR-016](/sops/cr-016)</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll get him to sign it by Friday — I’ll put it on his desk with a sticky note.',quality:'fail',label:'If the PI hasn’t read it, a signature is meaningless',
          feedback:'<p>If Dr. Martin hasn’t read the report, getting his signature by Friday means asking him to sign a document he hasn’t reviewed. His signature attests that he has read it, agrees with the findings, and accepts the action items. Signing unread documents is a data integrity issue and one of the most common PI oversight findings.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.3.1 · GCP Principle 10 · ALCOA+ · [SOP-CR-013](/sops/cr-013)</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll talk to him — he’s been busy but I’m sure he can review it this week.',quality:'partial',label:'Three previous promises didn’t work',
          feedback:'<p>“I’m sure he can review it” is an optimistic commitment based on three previous failures. You’re not giving James a firm date, and you’re not addressing the underlying problem. If he doesn’t review it by Friday, you’ll be back on another call making the same promise.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-013 · [SOP-CR-016](/sops/cr-016)</span>'}
       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 3 — The withdrawal',
       narration:'James raises a final item.',
       speaker:'James',
       dialogue:'<p>Participant 044 withdrew consent eight weeks ago. The withdrawal form only says “participant withdrew consent.” It doesn’t specify whether she withdrew from procedures only or also from data use. Since she completed all visits before withdrawing, can we assume she’s fine with her data being used in the final analysis?</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'She completed all her visits — I think it’s safe to assume she just didn’t want to come back. Include her data.',quality:'fail',label:'You cannot assume the scope of an unspecified withdrawal',
          feedback:'<p>“Participant withdrew consent” without specifying scope is an incomplete record. Assuming the narrowest interpretation to preserve the data is convenient but not supported by the documentation. If the participant intended to withdraw her data and it was included in the submission, that’s both a consent violation and a regulatory problem.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.9.1 · TCPS2 Art. 3.1 · Loi 25 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008)</span>'},
         {text:'The withdrawal form doesn’t specify the scope, so we don’t know her intent. We need to contact her to clarify — a brief, respectful call. I can have the CRC reach out this week.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>The withdrawal conversation should have clarified the scope at the time — but it didn’t, and the fix is to go back and ask. A brief, non-pressuring call that respects her decision and simply asks for clarification is appropriate. If she’s unreachable, consult the REB on how to handle the ambiguity.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.9.1 · TCPS2 Art. 3.1 · Loi 25 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008) §5.1.16</span>'},
         {text:'Let me check the consent form — it might have a clause about data use after withdrawal that covers this.',quality:'partial',label:'A reasonable first step, but not sufficient',
          feedback:'<p>Checking the ICF is reasonable — many forms include language about data retention after withdrawal. But even if the ICF says data may be retained, the participant’s actual stated wishes at the time of withdrawal take precedence. If she said “I want everything removed” and the CRC only documented “withdr consent,” the ICF clause doesn’t resolve the ambiguity.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">TCPS2 Art. 3.1 · ICH E6(R3) §2.9.1 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008)</span>'}
       ]}
    ],
    endings:{
      3:{label:'Strong',title:'You managed close-out pressure without cutting corners.',text:'Independent PI assessment of a major deviation, genuine PI review of monitoring reports, and proper resolution of an ambiguous withdrawal — you kept all three clean.'},
      2:{label:'Competent with gaps',title:'You handled most of the close-out correctly.',text:'One decision could create a problem in the regulatory file. Close-out errors are particularly costly because they go directly into the submission archive.'},
      1:{label:'Needs development',title:'The deadline pressure drove too many of your decisions.',text:'Close-out feels administrative, but the signatures, assessments, and records you finalize now become the permanent regulatory record.'},
      0:{label:'Critical concern',title:'A deviation closed on the sponsor’s assessment, an unread report signed, and an ambiguous withdrawal resolved by assumption.',text:'Each of these would be an audit finding. The sponsor’s August deadline is their constraint, not your standard.'}
    }
  },

  /* -- SCENARIO 6: Data Analyst — The Query Storm -- */
  {
    id:'query-storm', role:'data',
    topic:'Data Integrity · Delegation · PI Oversight',
    title:'The Query Storm',
    desc:'140 queries, 48 hours to database lock. Some of them aren’t yours to answer.',
    scenes:3, ref:'[SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014), 002', live:true,
    intro:'Database lock is Monday. It’s Thursday afternoon and the sponsor has just sent 140 data queries. The PI is in clinic all day Friday.',
    learner:{name:'Léa',role:'Data Analyst on a Phase III respiratory trial. Database lock is Monday.'},
    npc:{name:'Sylvie',role:'Project Manager. Not malicious, just under extreme deadline pressure. She’s been told the lock cannot slip.'},
    pressurePoints:[
      {label:'Pressure point 1 — The clinical queries',
       narration:'Sylvie comes to your desk with the query list. About 90 are administrative. The other 50 are clinical — AE grades, con-med classifications, eligibility re-confirmations.',
       speaker:'Sylvie',
       dialogue:'<p>Can you answer the clinical ones based on what’s in the source records? Dr. Pellerin is in clinic until 5 tomorrow. He can review your answers Monday morning before the lock.</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'Sure — I can see the source records and most of these are straightforward. I’ll flag anything I’m unsure about for Dr. Pellerin.',quality:'fail',label:'Clinical queries require clinical judgement',
          feedback:'<p>AE grading, relatedness assessment, and eligibility confirmation are physician responsibilities that cannot be delegated to a data analyst regardless of access to source records. Even if Dr. Pellerin reviews Monday, the query responses will be timestamped with your name — not the date the PI reviewed them.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.3.2 · [SOP-CR-002](/sops/cr-002) · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014) · ALCOA+</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll handle all 90 administrative queries today and tomorrow. For the clinical ones, I’ll triage them and prepare a summary sheet so Dr. Pellerin can work through them efficiently — but I can’t answer them myself. Can we get him 30 minutes tomorrow between patients?',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’ve separated what’s in your scope from what isn’t, and proposed a practical path forward. Thirty minutes of focused PI time with a prepared summary is often enough. If some queries remain open, a short lock delay is better than responses answered outside the proper delegation.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-002 · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014) · ICH E6(R3) §2.3.1</span>'},
         {text:'I can answer the ones where the source record is clear-cut — like a con-med that’s obviously an analgesic.',quality:'partial',label:'The issue is scope, not difficulty',
          feedback:'<p>The problem isn’t whether the answer is obvious — it’s whether you’re the right person to provide it. Con-med classification in a clinical trial involves checking against prohibited medications and assessing interactions with the study drug. The query was sent because the sponsor needs a documented clinical determination, not a data analyst’s reading of the source.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-002 · ICH E6(R3) §2.3.2</span>'}
       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 2 — The discrepancy',
       narration:'While working through administrative queries, you find one that’s not administrative. The source shows systolic BP 162 mmHg; the eCRF shows 126 — a transposition. The protocol says systolic above 160 triggers a safety review. If 162 is correct, a safety review was missed.',
       speaker:'Sylvie',
       dialogue:'<p>Just correct the eCRF to match the source. It’s clearly a typo — the digits are transposed.</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'You’re right — 162 is in the source, so I’ll update the eCRF and close the query.',quality:'fail',label:'The data correction is right; ignoring the safety implication isn’t',
          feedback:'<p>Correcting the eCRF to match the source is the right data correction. But if the correct value is 162, a protocol-specified safety review should have been triggered and wasn’t. Simply fixing the number without flagging the missed safety review means the data is now correct but the oversight failure is buried.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §2.5.2 · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014)</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll correct the eCRF and add a note in the query response that the value may have had safety implications. The PI can follow up after lock.',quality:'partial',label:'Flagging is good; deferring to post-lock is not',
          feedback:'<p>Noting the safety implication is better than ignoring it. But “the PI can follow up after lock” defers a potential deviation to a point where the database is frozen and corrections require a formal process. If this is a protocol deviation, it should be documented before lock.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · [SOP-CR-026](/sops/cr-026)</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll correct the eCRF to 162 — but this is bigger than a typo. A systolic above 160 should have triggered a safety review that didn’t happen. I need to flag this to Dr. Pellerin before we close this query.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’ve done both jobs: fixing the data error and identifying the clinical consequence. The missed safety review is a separate issue that requires PI assessment — was the participant harmed? Is this a protocol deviation? These questions need to be answered before database lock, not after.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · [SOP-CR-026](/sops/cr-026) · ICH E6(R3) §2.5.2</span>'}
       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 3 — The value you doubt',
       narration:'Near the end of the administrative queries: participant 112’s baseline potassium is 2.8 mEq/L. Source says 2.8, eCRF says 2.8. They match. But every other potassium across 12 months is between 4.0 and 4.5. A potassium of 2.8 is severely low. There’s no clinical note about it.',
       speaker:'Sylvie',
       dialogue:'<p>The source says 2.8, the eCRF says 2.8. They match. Confirm it and move on.</p>',
       choices:[
         {text:'The values match, but this doesn’t make clinical sense — every other potassium is 4.0 to 4.5 and there’s no note addressing a critically low value. I want to check the original lab report before confirming, and the PI needs to see this.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',
          feedback:'<p>You’re doing what a good data analyst does: not just matching numbers but recognizing when a value doesn’t fit the clinical picture. The next step is to pull the original lab report — the value may have been transcribed incorrectly. Either way, the PI needs to review it before the database locks.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §4 · ALCOA+ · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014)</span>'},
         {text:'They match — confirmed.',quality:'fail',label:'Source-to-eCRF concordance is not the only check',
          feedback:'<p>A potassium of 2.8 in a participant whose values are consistently 4.0–4.5 is a clinically significant outlier that should have been flagged and assessed. The absence of any clinical notation suggests either the value was never reviewed or the value itself is wrong — transcription from the lab, wrong patient’s result, unit error. Confirming without investigation means a potentially erroneous value enters the locked database.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">ICH E6(R3) §4 · ALCOA+ · [SOP-CR-014](/sops/cr-014)</span>'},
         {text:'I’ll confirm the match but add a data note flagging the discrepancy with the participant’s other values. Someone can review it post-lock.',quality:'partial',label:'Better than ignoring it, but check the lab report now',
          feedback:'<p>Flagging it is better than confirming blindly. But “confirm and note” means the value enters the locked database as confirmed, with a note saying “this might be wrong.” Post-lock corrections require a formal process. Check the original lab report now — it takes five minutes and could prevent a data integrity issue in the submission.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-014 · ICH E6(R3) §4</span>'}
       ]}
    ],
    endings:{
      3:{label:'Strong',title:'You maintained clear scope boundaries and caught what mattered.',text:'Clinical queries stayed with the physician, a missed safety review was surfaced, and a clinically implausible value was investigated before it entered the locked database.'},
      2:{label:'Competent with gaps',title:'You handled most of the pressure well.',text:'One decision could have let a problem slip into the locked database. The queries that look routine are sometimes the ones that matter most.'},
      1:{label:'Needs development',title:'The database lock deadline shaped too many of your decisions.',text:'The queries you answered, confirmed, or deferred each carry your name in the audit trail.'},
      0:{label:'Critical concern',title:'Clinical queries answered outside your delegation, a missed safety review buried, and an implausible value confirmed without investigation.',text:'The locked database becomes the regulatory record. What you confirm today is what the submission contains.'}
    }
  },

  /* -- SCENARIO 7: CRC -- The Consent Conversation -- */
  {
    id:'consent-conversation', role:'crc',
    topic:'Consent · Capacity · Quebec Civil Code',
    title:'The Consent Conversation',
    desc:'A participant shows signs of cognitive decline. Her daughter wants to keep her enrolled. Quebec law has specific rules here.',
    scenes:3, ref:'[SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008), CCQ Art. 21/24', live:true,
    intro:'Mrs. Ouellet, 74, enrolled 8 months ago when her cognition was intact. At today’s visit, she seems noticeably confused. Her daughter Julie is in the waiting room.',
    learner:{name:'Sophie',role:'CRC on a 12-month observational study of cognitive decline in older adults.'},
    npc:{name:'Julie Ouellet',role:'Mrs. Ouellet’s daughter. Caring, involved, wants her mother to continue.'},
    pressurePoints:[
      {label:'Pressure point 1 — The assessment',narration:'Mrs. Ouellet scores significantly worse on the cognitive assessment. When you ask about the study, she says: “What is this for again? I forget why I come here.” You step out to speak with Julie.',speaker:'Julie',dialogue:'<p>She’s been having more bad days lately, but she still wants to come — she told me so this morning.</p>',choices:[
        {text:'I need to flag this to the PI. Her score dropped and she can’t explain why she’s here — we need a formal capacity reassessment before any study procedures today.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',feedback:'<p>You’ve identified the trigger: a meaningful cognitive decline combined with statements suggesting she doesn’t understand her participation. The PI needs to conduct a formal capacity assessment before any further procedures.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">TCPS2 Art. 3.7 · CCQ Art. 21 · ICH E6(R3) §2.8.1 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008)</span>'},
        {text:'Julie says she wants to continue, and she did consent at baseline — I’ll document the visit and note the lower score.',quality:'fail',label:'Baseline consent does not cover indefinite future',feedback:'<p>Mrs. Ouellet’s statement is a significant indicator that she may no longer understand what she consented to. TCPS2 Article 3.7 requires capacity reassessment when there is reason to believe it has changed. Proceeding without reassessment means continuing with a participant whose capacity is in question.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">TCPS2 Art. 3.7 · ICH E6(R3) §2.8.1 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008)</span>'},
        {text:'I’ll explain the study to her again and if she says she wants to continue, that’s good enough.',quality:'partial',label:'Re-explaining is not a capacity assessment',feedback:'<p>Re-explaining is part of a capacity assessment, but “if she says she wants to continue” is not sufficient. Capacity means she understands the research, the risks, and what participation involves — not just that she’s willing to be here.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">TCPS2 Art. 3.7 · CCQ Art. 21 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008)</span>'}
      ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 2 — The daughter',narration:'The PI determines Mrs. Ouellet does not currently have capacity to consent. You discuss next steps with Julie.',speaker:'Julie',dialogue:'<p>I’m her daughter and I have power of attorney. I want her to stay in the study — the visits give her structure. Can I consent for her?</p>',choices:[
        {text:'You have power of attorney, so yes — you can sign on her behalf and we’ll continue.',quality:'fail',label:'Power of attorney does not automatically cover research',feedback:'<p>Power of attorney for care does not automatically authorize research participation under Quebec law. Civil Code Article 21 requires authorization by a mandatary, tutor, or curator AND approval by the Minister’s designated REB. A general power of attorney may not cover research participation.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">CCQ Art. 21 · TCPS2 Art. 3.9 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008) §7</span>'},
        {text:'Let me check the protocol — if it allows substitute consent, we can continue with your signature.',quality:'partial',label:'Protocol must comply with Quebec law',feedback:'<p>Checking the protocol is reasonable. But many sponsor protocols define “legally authorized representative” using US definitions that may not align with Quebec law. In Quebec, substitute consent for research requires a mandatary, tutor, or curator under Article 21. And Article 24 says if the participant objects, the objection overrides proxy consent.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">CCQ Art. 21, 24 · TCPS2 Art. 3.9 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008) §7</span>'},
        {text:'I appreciate that you’re looking out for her. But under Quebec law, research consent for someone who has lost capacity has specific requirements — I need to check with the PI and our REB about what your power of attorney covers.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',feedback:'<p>You’re not dismissing Julie — you’re being transparent that there’s a legal process. The key question is whether her mandate specifically covers research participation, and whether the REB approved this protocol for incapacitated participants with a substitute consent plan.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">CCQ Art. 21 · TCPS2 Art. 3.9 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008) §7</span>'}
      ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 3 — The objection',narration:'While you’re explaining the process, Mrs. Ouellet says clearly: “I don’t want any more tests. I want to go home.”',speaker:'Julie',dialogue:'<p>She doesn’t know what she’s saying. She was fine this morning — she told me she wanted to come.</p>',choices:[
        {text:'She did say she wanted to come this morning — Julie knows her better than we do. Let’s finish the visit.',quality:'fail',label:'A participant’s objection overrides proxy consent',feedback:'<p>Under Civil Code Article 24, if a person incapable of consenting objects to participation, that objection must be respected — even if a valid proxy has consented. “I don’t want any more tests” is an objection. Julie’s recollection of this morning does not override what Mrs. Ouellet is saying now.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">CCQ Art. 24 · TCPS2 Art. 3.9 · [SOP-CR-008](/sops/cr-008)</span>'},
        {text:'I hear you, Julie, and I know this is difficult. But your mother just said she doesn’t want any more tests. Under Quebec law, even when someone else is authorized to consent, the participant’s own objection has to be respected. We need to stop today’s procedures.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',feedback:'<p>You’ve acknowledged Julie’s perspective without dismissing her, but you’ve been clear about the legal requirement. Article 24 is unambiguous. Stopping today doesn’t mean permanent withdrawal — it means today’s visit cannot continue over her objection.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">CCQ Art. 24 · TCPS2 Art. 3.9 · ICH E6(R3) §2.8.1</span>'},
        {text:'Let’s take a break and see if she feels differently in 30 minutes.',quality:'partial',label:'Risks becoming “wait until she stops objecting”',feedback:'<p>A break isn’t inherently wrong, but “let’s see if she feels differently” risks becoming “let’s wait until she stops objecting.” The safer path is to respect the objection now and discuss next steps with the PI.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">CCQ Art. 24 · TCPS2 Art. 3.9</span>'}
      ]}
    ],
    endings:{
      3:{label:'Strong',title:'You identified a capacity concern, navigated Quebec’s substitute consent requirements, and respected a participant’s objection.',text:'Consent in cognitive decline studies is one of the most legally complex areas in clinical research. The Quebec Civil Code requirements are specific and differ from what many protocols assume.'},
      2:{label:'Competent with gaps',title:'You got most of this right, but one decision left a gap.',text:'Review the Civil Code requirements: Article 21 for substitute consent, Article 24 for participant objection.'},
      1:{label:'Needs development',title:'The personal dynamics shaped your decisions more than the legal framework did.',text:'A caring daughter, a participant who seemed willing this morning — none of these override the Civil Code.'},
      0:{label:'Critical concern',title:'You continued procedures with questionable capacity, accepted proxy consent without verification, and proceeded over an expressed objection.',text:'Each is a serious consent violation under Quebec law.'}
    }
  },

  /* -- SCENARIO 8: PI -- The Sponsor-Investigator Study -- */
  {
    id:'sponsor-inv-study', role:'pi',
    topic:'Sponsor-Investigator Obligations · IP Management · Safety Reporting',
    title:'The Sponsor-Investigator Study',
    desc:'You hold the CTA for your own trial. Your CRC just surfaced a backlog of compliance failures. Nobody is watching you the way a sponsor would.',
    scenes:3, ref:'[SOP-CR-018](/sops/cr-018), 010, 012', live:true,
    intro:'You are running your own investigator-initiated Phase II trial. You hold the CTA. 14 participants, single site. Your CIHR grant runs out in 4 months.',
    learner:{name:'Dr. Caron',role:'PI and sponsor-investigator on a Phase II trial of a repurposed drug for a rare autoimmune condition.'},
    npc:{name:'Amélie',role:'Your CRC. Competent and direct. Not afraid to tell you when something is wrong.'},
    pressurePoints:[
      {label:'Pressure point 1 — The safety backlog',narration:'Amélie comes into your office with a list.',speaker:'Amélie',dialogue:'<p>I’ve been going through the safety files. There are three SAEs from the past two months that were reported to the REB on time, but the two that qualify as SUSARs were never reported to Health Canada. You’re the sponsor — that reporting goes through you. The 15-day window has passed for both.</p>',choices:[
        {text:'You’re right — I dropped the ball. Let me submit both this week with the correct dates and a cover letter explaining the delay. And we need a process so this doesn’t happen again.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',feedback:'<p>You’ve acknowledged the gap, committed to honest late reporting, and identified the systemic issue. Late SUSAR reports are a compliance failure, but they’re correctable. The root cause is likely that you don’t have a pharmacovigilance process — as sponsor-investigator, you need to build one.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">C.05.014 · [SOP-CR-012](/sops/cr-012) · ICH E6(R3) §2.7</span>'},
        {text:'They were reported to the REB — that’s the main thing. REB reporting and Health Canada reporting are separate obligations that run in parallel. Submit them now, accurately dated, with an explanation for the delay.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">C.05.014 · Division 5 · Food and Drug Act · [SOP-CR-012](/sops/cr-012)</span>'},
        {text:'Can you handle the Health Canada submissions? You know the details better than I do.',quality:'partial',label:'Amélie can prepare them, but the responsibility is yours',feedback:'<p>Amélie can prepare the reports, but you need to review, sign, and submit them. As sponsor-investigator, the SUSAR reports bear your name and your CTA number.       ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 2 — The labelling error',narration:'Amélie brings you something else.',speaker:'Amélie',dialogue:'<p>The last batch of study drug — the labels show the previous protocol version number.         {text:'The drug itself hasn’t changed — same formulation, same dose. The label version is administrative. Update the remaining stock and move on.',quality:'fail',label:'IP labelling is a regulatory requirement',feedback:'<p>IP labelling is a regulatory requirement under Division 5. You need to assess impact on the four affected participants, document the error, and file a deviation report.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">C.05.010(d) · [SOP-CR-010](/sops/cr-010) · ICH E6(R3) §2.10</span>'},
        {text:'That’s a problem. I need to assess impact, document it, notify the REB, and correct the labels. Pull together the dispensing records for the four affected participants.',quality:'good',label:'Correct',feedback:'<p>The labelling error is a protocol deviation that needs documentation, impact assessment, and reporting. The formulation and dose haven’t changed, so the safety impact is likely low — but that assessment needs to be made formally, not assumed.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-010 · [SOP-CR-026](/sops/cr-026) · C.05.010(d) · ICH E6(R3) §2.10</span>'},
        {text:'Ask the pharmacy to relabel the remaining stock and add a note to each file. We don’t need to report it — the drug is correct.',quality:'partial',label:'Relabelling is not the whole response',feedback:'<p>Relabelling is part of the corrective action but not all of it. A labelling error affecting four participants is a protocol deviation. As sponsor-investigator, an IP deviation on your study reflects directly on your sponsor obligations.</p><span class="jc-fb-ref">SOP-CR-010 · [SOP-CR-026](/sops/cr-026) · C.05.010(d)</span>'}
      ]},
      {label:'Pressure point 3 — The grant',narration:'Amélie raises the bigger picture.',speaker:'Amélie',dialogue:'<p>The grant runs out in four months. Do we pause enrolment until we catch up, or keep going?</p>',choices:[
        {text:'We can’t pause — we’ll lose the funding and never finish.
